<a id="ti:large-group-error"></a>
Put $u_P=u/u_*$. We first discard $u_P>x^{b_*/4}$. For $u\asymp x$, the part of $2u+1$ supported on primes exceeding $W$ has at most $O(L/\log W)=O(\sqrt L)$ prime factors with multiplicity. Its number of divisors is consequently at most $\exp(O(\sqrt L))$, since $a+1\le2^a$ for $a\ge1$. This bounds the possible rough divisors $m$. The tail bound in Lemma <a href="#wt:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wt:tail">17</a>, with $d=2/5$, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{\substack{mn=2u+1,\ u\asymp x\\u_P>x^{b_*/4}}}
          |\alpha_m\beta_n|A_0(u)|\Psi(u/x)|\notag\\
 &\qquad\ll
 xL^{O(1)}\exp(O(\sqrt L))
   \left(\sum_v\frac{a(v)}v\right)
   \sum_{\substack{r>x^{b_*/4}\\r_*=1}}\frac{W_1(r)}r
 \ll xL^{O(1)}
       \exp\left(-\frac{b_*}{4}L^{1-d}+O(\sqrt L)\right).
 \label{ti:large-group-error}
\end{aligned}$$ To obtain the first inequality, write $u=vr$, use $vr\asymp x$ to bound $1\ll x/(vr)$, and then drop the product restriction. The harmonic ordinary-slot mass is $H_Q=L^{O(1)}$. Since $1-d>1/2$, <a href="#ti:large-group-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:large-group-error">[ti:large-group-error]</a> is $O_H(xL^{-H})$ for every fixed $H$. The same conclusion will hold whenever we reinstate these discarded tuples with a multiplier of absolute value at most one.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### A smooth allocation with bounded residual.

Set <a id="ti:E"></a>$$\label{ti:E}
                            E=UL^{-K_0},$$ where the fixed positive constant $K_0$ will be chosen below. Put the entire group-prime part and the designated prime into $h$. Process all remaining ordinary slots by increasing band index, in a fixed order within each band, assigning each slot either to $e$ or to $h$. Write these slots as $p_1,\ldots,p_N$, with band indices $j(i)$; here $N=r_0(j_x+1)-1=O(T)$.

Fix a smooth function $\gamma:\mathbb R\to[0,1]$ equal to zero on $(-\infty,0]$ and to one on $[1,\infty)$. At slot $i$, with residual target $R_i$, assign the prime to $e$ with weight $$\gamma\left(\frac{R_i-\log p_i}{s_{j(i)}}\right)$$ and to $h$ with the complementary weight. Start with $R_1=\log E$, and subtract $\log p_i$ from the residual precisely when the slot is assigned to $e$.

<a id="ti:allocation-invariant"></a>
For a tuple surviving the preceding deletion, the product withheld from allocation is at most $x^{b_*/2}$. Since $u\asymp x$ and $U\le x^{1-b_*}$, its available logarithms satisfy, for large $x$, $$0<\log E\le\sum_{i=1}^N\log p_i.$$ Every branch of positive weight has the invariant $$\label{ti:allocation-invariant}
 0\le R_i\le\sum_{t=i}^N\log p_t+(c_2+1)s_{j_x}
                    \qquad(1\le i\le N+1).$$ Indeed, taking a slot requires $R_i>\log p_i$, so preserves both nonnegativity and the upper bound after subtracting that logarithm. Skipping a slot in band $j$ requires $$R_i<\log p_i+s_j\le(c_2+1)s_j.$$ If $j<j_x$, the next band has at least $r_0-1$ available slots and therefore total logarithm at least $(r_0-1)c_1s_j/2\ge(c_2+1)s_j$. These slots are all still unprocessed. If $j=j_x$, the error term in <a href="#ti:allocation-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:allocation-invariant">[ti:allocation-invariant]</a> supplies the bound directly. This proves the invariant by induction, including the terminal step.

<a id="ti:e-range"></a>
The geometric sum of all unprocessed band scales is $O(s_{j(i)})$. Thus <a href="#ti:allocation-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:allocation-invariant">[ti:allocation-invariant]</a> also places every argument $(R_i-\log p_i)/s_{j(i)}$ in one fixed compact interval, independently of $K_0$. At termination, $$0\le\log(E/e)=R_{N+1}\le(c_2+1)s_{j_x}.$$ Choose once and for all $C_s>40(c_2+1)$. Every surviving branch has $$\label{ti:e-range}
                         EL^{-C_s}\le e\le E.$$ This choice of $C_s$ is independent of $K_0$.

Choose a fixed smooth $\rho:\mathbb R\to[0,1]$ of compact support which equals one throughout the possible argument interval, and put $$f_1=\rho\gamma,\qquad f_0=\rho(1-\gamma).$$ The allocation is unchanged on the retained tuples. On any tuple the sum of its full branch weights is at most one, since $f_0+f_1=\rho\le1$ at every node of the choice tree. We may consequently impose <a href="#ti:e-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:e-range">[ti:e-range]</a> and reinstate all discarded tuples, with the same negligible error <a href="#ti:large-group-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:large-group-error">[ti:large-group-error]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### Separation and its uniform cost.

<a id="ti:fourier-cost"></a>
<a id="ti:fourier-tail"></a>
Use the Fourier convention $$\widehat f(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R}f(t)e^{-i\xi t}\,dt,
 \qquad f(t)=\int_{\mathbb R}\widehat f(\xi)e^{i\xi t}
                                    \frac{d\xi}{2\pi},$$ and set $$C_F=\max\left(1,
       \int|\widehat f_0(\xi)|\frac{d\xi}{2\pi},
       \int|\widehat f_1(\xi)|\frac{d\xi}{2\pi}\right).$$ For a fixed branch $\delta=(\delta_1,\ldots,\delta_N)\in\{0,1\}^N$, Fourier inversion of its $N$ transitions has total variation at most $C_F^N$. Summing over the branches costs at most $$\label{ti:fourier-cost}
                         (2C_F)^N\le L^{C_f}$$ for a fixed exponent, increased below to include the fixed cutoff $\Psi$. In particular this exponent is independent of $K_0$. Truncating each transition variable at $|\xi_i|\le L$ has total error at most $$\label{ti:fourier-tail}
                  2^N N C_M C_F^{N-1}L^{-M}$$ for any fixed $M$, by the union bound on the complementary integration regions and the rapid decay of a single Fourier factor. The constant $C_M$ occurs only once in each term of this estimate. Increasing $M$ therefore does not increase the exponent arising from $C_F^N$.

To see the separated phases explicitly, use $R_i=\log E-\sum_{t<i}\delta_t\log p_t$. The product of the Fourier phases is <a id="ti:slot-frequencies"></a>$$\label{ti:slot-frequencies}
 \exp\left(i\log E\sum_{i=1}^N\frac{\xi_i}{s_{j(i)}}\right)
       \prod_{t=1}^N p_t^{i\sigma_t},\qquad
 \sigma_t=-\frac{\xi_t}{s_{j(t)}}
           -\delta_t\sum_{i>t}\frac{\xi_i}{s_{j(i)}}.$$ The dependence on $E$ is entirely in the scalar of modulus one. Since $\sum_i s_{j(i)}^{-1}=O(T^{-1})$, the retained frequencies satisfy $|\sigma_t|=O(L/T)$. Fourier inversion of the fixed smooth function $t\mapsto\Psi(e^t)$ likewise separates $\Psi(eh/x)$, with bounded integral norm; truncation at a fixed power of $L$ has arbitrary logarithmic accuracy. Its contribution is a factor $h^{i\varrho}$, a factor $e^{i\varrho}$ absorbed into the coefficient of $e$, and a scalar of modulus one.

All these errors can be summed before Cauchy–Schwarz. The original $m,n$ restrictions still imply $u\asymp x$, and the unmodified factorization mass is bounded by $$\sum_{\substack{mn=2u+1\\u\asymp x}}
       |\alpha_m\beta_n|A_0(u)
       \ll L^{O(1)}\sum_{v\asymp x}\tau(v)
       \ll xL^{O(1)}.$$ Here and below $\tau$ is the divisor function. The same estimate applies to the labelled-slot sum with its original factorial normalization. Combining it with <a href="#ti:fourier-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:fourier-tail">[ti:fourier-tail]</a> proves an error $O_H(xL^{-H})$ for arbitrary fixed $H$, without changing the fixed exponent in <a href="#ti:fourier-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:fourier-cost">[ti:fourier-cost]</a>.

<a id="ti:separated-bilinear"></a>
Consequently the required sum is, up to these errors, a sum and integral of total variation at most $L^{C_f}$ of expressions $$\label{ti:separated-bilinear}
 \begin{split}
 B'&=\sum_{e,n}a_e(e)\beta_n
                  \sum_{mn=2eh+1}\alpha_m a_h(h),\\
 a_h(h)&=h^{i\varrho}\varepsilon(h)W_1(h)H(h_*),\qquad
 H(t)=\sum_{pr=t}\mathbf{1}_{p\in Q_{j_{**}}}p^{i\sigma}c_r.
 \end{split}$$ The original $m,n$ restrictions are in $\alpha,\beta$, and the $e$ range <a href="#ti:e-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:e-range">[ti:e-range]</a> is in $a_e$. The prime in $H$ is the designated prime. Its phase can be taken to be zero at this stage; we allow a bounded-power frequency $\sigma$ in the notation. All phases in <a href="#ti:separated-bilinear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:separated-bilinear">[ti:separated-bilinear]</a> have fixed logarithmic-power bounds.

<a id="ti:coefficient-bounds"></a>
Both $a_e$ and $c_r$ are bounded coefficients. In fact, for a fixed branch and a fixed product, disjointness of the bands determines the prime multiset in every band. There are at most $r_0!$ assignments to the relevant labelled slots of any one band, even when primes repeat. Distributing the original factorial normalizations between the two coefficients only improves the resulting bound $$\label{ti:coefficient-bounds}
 |a_e(e)|,|c_r|\le(r_0!)^{j_x+1}\le L^{C_a},
 \qquad |a_h(h)|\le L^{C_h}\tau(h_*).$$ The last bound comes from summing over the designated prime divisor of $h_*$. The exponents $C_a,C_h$ are fixed independently of $K_0$ and of the Fourier error accuracy. Only ordinary-band primes were assigned to $e$, so its removal leaves the marked group weight and the sign exactly on $h$, as used in <a href="#ti:separated-bilinear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:separated-bilinear">[ti:separated-bilinear]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### Cauchy–Schwarz and the diagonal.

<a id="ti:cauchy"></a>
Choose bounded nonnegative smooth majorants $\eta_e,\eta_n$, equal to at least one on the $e,n$ ranges. We can arrange $$\operatorname{supp}\eta_e\subset[\tfrac12L^{-C_s},2],
 \qquad \operatorname{supp}\eta_n\subset[1/2,3],
 \qquad
 \|\eta_e^{(j)}\|_\infty+\|\eta_n^{(j)}\|_\infty
                           \ll_j L^{C_{\eta}(j+1)},$$ where $C_{\eta}$ depends only on $C_s$. There are $O(EV)$ pairs $(e,n)$ in the original ranges. Hence <a href="#ti:coefficient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:coefficient-bounds">[ti:coefficient-bounds]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\label{ti:cauchy}
 |B'|^2\ll EVL^{C_p}\mathcal N,\qquad
 \mathcal N=\sum_{e,n}\eta_e(e/E)\eta_n(n/V)
                \left|\sum_{mn=2eh+1}\alpha_m a_h(h)\right|^2.$$ Here $C_p$ is fixed independently of $K_0$.

<a id="ti:diagonal"></a>
<a id="ti:K-choice"></a>
The diagonal $m=m'$ in the square forces $h=h'$. Collecting by $v=mn$ and then using <a href="#ti:coefficient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:coefficient-bounds">[ti:coefficient-bounds]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal N_{\mathrm{diag}}
 &\ll L^{C_d}\sum_{\substack{v\asymp x\\v\ {\rm odd}}}
       \tau(v)\sum_{e\mid(v-1)/2}
                   \tau\left(\frac{v-1}{2e}\right)^2\notag\\
 &\le L^{C_d}\sum_{v\asymp x}\tau(v)\tau(v-1)^3
 \ll xL^{C_d'}.
 \label{ti:diagonal}
\end{aligned}$$ The final estimate follows from Cauchy–Schwarz and the fixed second and sixth moments of $\tau$ in Proposition <a href="#pre:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:moments">3</a>. Enlarging the $e$ divisor set in this calculation makes $C_d'$ independent of $K_0$. Since $EV\asymp xL^{-K_0}$, the diagonal contribution to the right side of <a href="#ti:cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:cauchy">[ti:cauchy]</a> is at most $x^2L^{-K_0+C_p+C_d'}$. We now fix $$\label{ti:K-choice}
                  K_0>2(D_*+C_f+1)+C_p+C_d'.$$ It remains to obtain an arbitrarily strong logarithmic saving for the off-diagonal part of $\mathcal N$ with this fixed $K_0$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### The off-diagonal change of variables.

<a id="ti:k-range"></a>
A pair of representations in the expanded square satisfies $$mn=2eh+1,\qquad m'n=2eh'+1.$$ The first identity gives $(n,2e)=1$. Subtracting the two identities therefore gives a unique integer $k$ with <a id="ti:determinant-forward"></a>$$\label{ti:determinant-forward}
 m'-m=2ek,\qquad h'-h=kn,\qquad
 z=m'h,\qquad z+k=mh'.$$ Indeed $mh'-m'h=k(mn-2eh)=k$. On the off-diagonal $k\ne0$, and the lower support bound for $e$ gives $$\label{ti:k-range}
                         0<|k|\ll L^{K_0+C_s}.$$

<a id="ti:determinant-reverse"></a>
For completeness, this parametrization is reversible for both signs of $k$. Start from positive factorizations $z=m'h$, $z+k=mh'$, require $m'\equiv m\pmod{2|k|}$, and put $$\label{ti:determinant-reverse}
               e=\frac{m'-m}{2k}>0,\qquad
               n=\frac{2eh+1}{m}.$$ The first quantity is an integer. The determinant identity implies $$m(h'-h)=k(2eh+1).$$ Since $P^-(m)>W$ and $k$ has the fixed logarithmic-power bound <a href="#ti:k-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:k-range">[ti:k-range]</a>, we have $(m,k)=1$ for large $x$. Thus $n$ is a positive integer and $h'-h=kn$. Substituting $m'=m+2ek$ recovers $m'n=2eh'+1$. Every reconstructed variable is unique. Applying the size cutoffs in <a href="#ti:cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:cauchy">[ti:cauchy]</a> therefore gives an exact bijection, with no additional multiplicity or divisibility condition.

<a id="ti:character-orthogonality"></a>
Both $m,m'$ are odd and coprime to $k$. With $q_k=2|k|$, their congruence is imposed exactly by $$\label{ti:character-orthogonality}
 \mathbf{1}_{m'\equiv m\ ({\rm mod}\ q_k)}
        =\frac1{\varphi(q_k)}\sum_{\chi\ ({\rm mod}\ q_k)}
                              \chi(m)\overline{\chi(m')}.$$ The normalized character sum has total coefficient mass one.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### The pulled-back cutoffs.

On the supports above, $h\asymp x/e$ and $m'\asymp U$. Thus $z\asymp Ux/e$ lies between fixed constant multiples of $xL^{K_0}$ and $xL^{K_0+C_s}$. Insert a smooth dyadic partition in $z$, retaining an outer cutoff $\psi_Z(z/Z)$ in every piece. Only $O(1+T)$ dyads are needed, and all have $Z=xL^{O(1)}$.

<a id="ti:pulled-derivatives"></a>
For a fixed $k,Z$, put $$t_1=\log(m/U),\quad t_2=\log(m'/U),\quad t_3=\log(z/Z).$$ The arguments of the two majorants are exactly <a id="ti:pulled-cutoffs"></a>$$\label{ti:pulled-cutoffs}
 \begin{split}
 \frac eE
    &=\frac{m'-m}{2kE}
      =\frac{U}{2kE}(e^{t_2}-e^{t_1}),\\
 \frac nV
    &=\frac1V\left(\frac{(m'-m)z}{kmm'}+\frac1m\right)\\
    &=\frac{Z}{kUV}e^{t_3}(e^{-t_1}-e^{-t_2})
                                      +\frac1{UV}e^{-t_1}.
 \end{split}$$ On a fixed compact box in these coordinates, every log-coordinate derivative of the first expression is $O_j(U/(|k|E))$, and every derivative of the second is $$O_j\left(\frac{Z}{|k|UV}+\frac1{UV}\right).$$ All these quantities are bounded by fixed powers of $L$ after <a href="#ti:K-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:K-choice">[ti:K-choice]</a>. The chain rule and the derivative bounds for $\eta_e,\eta_n$ therefore give, for each multi-index $\nu$, $$\label{ti:pulled-derivatives}
             \|\partial^\nu b_{k,Z}\|_\infty
                         \ll_\nu L^{C_0(|\nu|+1)}$$ for the pulled-back product of majorants, multiplied by fixed smooth buffers in $t_1,t_2,t_3$. Choose these buffers to equal one on the original $m,m'$ dyads and on the support of the outer $z$ cutoff. This places $b_{k,Z}$ on a fixed compact box and leaves the sequence $\alpha$ unchanged. The majorants vanish in neighborhoods of nonpositive $e/E$ and $n/V$, so extending the product by zero across those regions is smooth. Positivity in <a href="#ti:determinant-reverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:determinant-reverse">[ti:determinant-reverse]</a> is thereby imposed by the same cutoffs.

<a id="ti:post-fourier-tail"></a>
This is the fixed-dimensional setting of Lemma <a href="#pre:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:separation">5</a>. Choose a fixed $C_8$ larger than the slope required in <a href="#ti:pulled-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:pulled-derivatives">[ti:pulled-derivatives]</a>, and put $P=L^{C_8}$. Repeated integration by parts in these three coordinates gives $$|\widehat b_{k,Z}(\boldsymbol\xi)|
       \ll_j(1+|\boldsymbol\xi|/P)^{-j},\qquad
 \int_{\mathbb R^3}|\widehat b_{k,Z}(\boldsymbol\xi)|
                                      \,d\boldsymbol\xi\ll P^3,$$ and $$\label{ti:post-fourier-tail}
 \int_{|\boldsymbol\xi|>PL}|\widehat b_{k,Z}(\boldsymbol\xi)|
                 \,d\boldsymbol\xi\ll_j P^3L^{3-j}.$$ Consequently the cutoffs separate into phases $m^{i\xi_1}(m')^{i\xi_2}z^{i\xi_3}$ at fixed logarithmic-power cost and with fixed logarithmic-power frequencies. Scalars involving $U$ and $Z$ have modulus one.

To justify the tails absolutely, an endpoint convolution is bounded by $L^{O(1)}\tau(v_*)^2$: there are at most $\tau_3(v_*)\le\tau(v_*)^2$ triples $mpr=v_*$. Including the bounded group weights and dropping the congruence, Cauchy–Schwarz and the fixed fourth divisor moment in Proposition <a href="#pre:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:moments">3</a> give <a id="ti:absolute-correlation"></a>$$\label{ti:absolute-correlation}
 L^{O(1)}\sum_{z\asymp Z}\tau(z)^2\tau(z+k)^2
                         \ll ZL^{O(1)}.$$ Here $|k|\ll L^{O(1)}=o(Z)$ and both positions are positive. This estimate controls <a href="#ti:post-fourier-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:post-fourier-tail">[ti:post-fourier-tail]</a>, after summing over all $k$ and $Z$, to arbitrary logarithmic accuracy by increasing $j$. The fixed exponent $C_8$ need not increase with that accuracy.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Identifying the two endpoints.

<a id="ti:separated-correlation"></a>
Fix one character in <a href="#ti:character-orthogonality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:character-orthogonality">[ti:character-orthogonality]</a> and one retained Fourier term. Before the character and Fourier factors, the coefficient from the expanded square is $$\alpha_m\overline{\alpha_{m'}}
                        a_h(h)\overline{a_h(h')}.$$ Define invariant cores, for positive integers $v$, by <a id="ti:endpoint-cores"></a>$$\label{ti:endpoint-cores}
 \begin{split}
 F_0(v)&=\sum_{mpr=v_*}\alpha_m\chi(m)
             m^{i(\varrho-\xi_2)}
             \mathbf{1}_{p\in Q_{j_{**}}}p^{-i\sigma}\overline{c_r},\\
 G_0(v)&=\sum_{mpr=v_*}\alpha_m\chi(m)
             m^{i(\xi_1+\varrho)}
             \mathbf{1}_{p\in Q_{j_{**}}}p^{-i\sigma}\overline{c_r}.
 \end{split}$$ Both cores contain the original restricted sequence $\alpha$. All group primes are below $W$, whereas $m,m'$ have no prime factor at most $W$. Therefore <a id="ti:group-identities"></a>$$\label{ti:group-identities}
 \begin{gathered}
 z_*=m'h_*,\qquad (z+k)_*=mh'_*,\\
 W_1(z)=W_1(h),\quad W_1(z+k)=W_1(h'),\qquad
 \varepsilon(z)=\varepsilon(h),\quad
 \varepsilon(z+k)=\varepsilon(h').
 \end{gathered}$$ Using also $h=z/m'$ and $h'=(z+k)/m$, direct expansion shows that the separated sum is precisely $$\label{ti:separated-correlation}
 \sum_{\substack{z>0\\z+k>0}}
  \psi_Z(z/Z)z^{i(\varrho+\xi_3)}(z+k)^{-i\varrho}
       \overline{F_0(z)}G_0(z+k)
       W_1(z)W_1(z+k)\varepsilon(z)\varepsilon(z+k).$$ For example, conjugating the first core supplies $\overline{\alpha_{m'}}\overline{\chi(m')}
(m')^{i(\xi_2-\varrho)}H(h_*)$, while the second core supplies $\alpha_m\chi(m)m^{i(\xi_1+\varrho)}\overline{H(h'_*)}$. The remaining powers in <a href="#ti:separated-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:separated-correlation">[ti:separated-correlation]</a> give exactly $h^{i\varrho}(h')^{-i\varrho}z^{i\xi_3}$. Thus the discrepancy-bearing first core has the required coefficient $\alpha$, with its conjugation occurring outside that core.

<a id="ti:correlation-bound"></a>
Every hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#sh:correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sh:correlation">12</a> is now satisfied. The designated slot is a prime in the fixed range <a href="#ti:designated-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:designated-range">[ti:designated-range]</a>; the residual coefficients are bounded by <a href="#ti:coefficient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:coefficient-bounds">[ti:coefficient-bounds]</a>; the character modulus $2|k|$ and all frequencies have fixed logarithmic-power bounds; and the two endpoints have the same allowed sign. Finally, set $$\widetilde\psi_Z(t)=t\psi_Z(t).$$ Then $\psi_Z(z/Z)=(Z/z)\widetilde\psi_Z(z/Z)$. Consequently <a href="#ti:separated-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:separated-correlation">[ti:separated-correlation]</a> is exactly $Z$ times a harmonic correlation covered by that theorem. For any prescribed $D_0$ it is therefore $$\label{ti:correlation-bound}
                              O(ZL^{-D_0}),$$ provided the discrepancy exponent $B$ is chosen sufficiently large after all the fixed bounds and $D_0$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Completing the parameter choices.

Here is an explicit ordering of the choices. First fix the weight geometry, $b_*,D_*,C$, the designated band, the allocation functions, and the cutoff. These determine the exponents $$C_s,\ C_a,\ C_h,\ C_f,\ C_p,\ C_d'.$$ Fix $K_0$ by <a href="#ti:K-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:K-choice">[ti:K-choice]</a>. This fixes the derivative and frequency bounds after Cauchy–Schwarz, the Fourier scale $P$, and exponents $K_k,K_z,C_{\rm sep}$ such that there are at most $L^{K_k}$ shifts, $Z\le xL^{K_z}$, and the total post-Cauchy separation cost, including the dyads, is at most $L^{C_{\rm sep}}$. These exponents are all fixed.

Choose $A_{\rm off}>0$ so that $$-K_0+C_p-A_{\rm off}<-2(D_*+C_f+1),$$ and then choose $$D_0>A_{\rm off}+K_k+K_z+C_{\rm sep}+2.$$ Choose the differentiation orders in the Fourier tail estimates large enough for the same error target. Theorem <a href="#sh:correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sh:correlation">12</a> now supplies a sufficiently large fixed $B$. Summing <a href="#ti:correlation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:correlation-bound">[ti:correlation-bound]</a> over shifts, dyads, characters, and the retained Fourier integrals gives $$|\mathcal N_{\rm off}|
                                  \ll xL^{-A_{\rm off}}.$$ Together with <a href="#ti:diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:diagonal">[ti:diagonal]</a>, <a href="#ti:K-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:K-choice">[ti:K-choice]</a>, and <a href="#ti:cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:cauchy">[ti:cauchy]</a>, this proves $|B'|\ll xL^{-D_*-C_f-1}$. The total pre-Cauchy variation $L^{C_f}$ and the arbitrarily small previously discarded errors prove <a href="#ti:bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ti:bound">[ti:bound]</a> for each choice of $\varepsilon$, and hence for $A$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ti-proxy-order"></a>
<div id="ti:proxy-order" class="remark">

*Remark 21* (Precision in subsequent proxy replacements). Theorem <a href="#ti:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ti:distribution">20</a> permits the actual interval restriction to be included in $\alpha$, and it asks only for boundedness of the other coefficient. Thus it can be applied a second time after exchanging the two factors, even if the first coefficient has already been replaced by a nonsmooth bounded proxy. On $O(L)$ contributing factor dyads, its total error is $O(xL^{-D_*+1})$. The lower bound $X_A\gg xL^{-C_A}$ in Lemma <a href="#wt:mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wt:mass">18</a> makes this $o(X_A/L)$ whenever $D_*>C_A+2$.

In particular, if a later proxy construction has coefficients bounded by one independently of its logarithmic cell precision $S_1$, use that coefficient bound when making all the choices above. First fix $K_0$, the shifted-correlation accuracy, and finally the discrepancy exponent $B$; only afterwards choose the proxy precision $S_1>2B+3$. Neither $C_f$ nor the coefficient or diagonal exponents depends on $S_1$. Increasing $S_1$ therefore changes only how large $x$ must be and does not reopen any earlier choice.

</div>

<a id="cg:section"></a>
### Distribution in congruence classes

<a id="cg:remainder"></a>
For an odd squarefree integer $d$ define $$\label{cg:remainder}
 r(d)=\sum_{\substack{u\ge1\\2u+1\equiv0\pmod d}}
                    A(u)\Psi(u/x)-\frac{X_A}{\varphi(d)}.$$ The following estimate supplies the distribution needed by the sieve. Its proof uses only the prime slot in $Q_0$, the weight bounds already proved, and the classical Siegel–Walfisz and multiplicative large sieve estimates in <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[SmoothShifted](#ref-SmoothShifted), Section ``Notation and preliminary estimates''].

<a id="stmt-cg-distribution"></a>
<div id="cg:distribution" class="theorem">

**Theorem 22** (Congruence distribution). *For every fixed $0<\vartheta<1/2$ and $D>0$, $$\sum_{\substack{d\le x^\vartheta\\d\ {\rm odd\ and\ squarefree}}}
       |r(d)|\ll_{\vartheta,D}xL^{-D}+X_A L^{-18}.$$ The constants may depend on the fixed weight geometry and $\Psi$, but not on any later proxy precision. No effective bound on the threshold for $x$ is asserted.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cg-distribution"><summary>Proof of stmt-cg-distribution</summary>

<a id="cg:characters"></a>
<a id="cg:totient-sum"></a>
*Proof.* Write $Q=x^\vartheta$ and $w(u)=A(u)\Psi(u/x)$. The congruence in Equation <a href="#cg:remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:remainder">[cg:remainder]</a> is the single unit class $a_d\equiv-2^{-1}\pmod d$. Character orthogonality gives the exact identity $$\label{cg:characters}
 r(d)=\frac1{\varphi(d)}
       \sum_{\substack{\chi\bmod d\\\chi\ne\chi_0}}
          \overline{\chi(a_d)}\sum_u w(u)\chi(u)
       -\frac1{\varphi(d)}\sum_{(u,d)>1}w(u).$$ We first bound the correction in the second term. Mertens’ estimate implies $$\label{cg:totient-sum}
 \sum_{h\le Q}\frac{\mu^2(h)}{\varphi(h)}
 \le\prod_{p\le Q}\left(1+\frac1{p-1}\right)\ll L.$$ For a fixed $u$ in the effective support of $w$, we have $x\le u\le2x$ and every prime divisor of $u$ is at least $L^{20}$. Writing a squarefree $d$ divisible by $p$ as $ph$, and then using a union bound, gives $$\sum_{\substack{d\le Q\\d\ {\rm squarefree}\\(d,u)>1}}
       \frac1{\varphi(d)}
 \le\sum_{p\mid u}\frac1{p-1}
        \sum_{h\le Q}\frac{\mu^2(h)}{\varphi(h)}
 \ll L\sum_{p\mid u}\frac1{p-1}\ll L^{-18}.$$ For the last inequality, $u$ has at most $\log(2x)/(20T)$ distinct prime divisors. Since $w\ge0$, the total principal correction is therefore $O(X_A L^{-18})$.

To treat the nonprincipal characters, delete one designated ordered slot of $Q_0$ from the definition of $a$. Retain the original divisor $(r_0!)^{j_x+1}$ in the resulting coefficient, denoted by $a^-(v)$. If the residual exponents are $e_p$, then $$a^-(v)=\frac1{r_0}\prod_{j=0}^{j_x}\prod_{p\in Q_j}\frac1{e_p!}
 \le\frac1{r_0},$$ with $r_0-1$ slots in $Q_0$ and $r_0$ slots in every other band, and $a^-(v)=0$ off this support. Partitioning ordered tuples by the deleted prime gives, including when primes repeat, $$a(v)=\sum_{\substack{p\in Q_0\\p\mid v}}a^-(v/p).$$ The $Q_0$-primes lie outside $\mathcal P$, so removing that slot changes neither $W_1$ nor $\mathcal E$. Consequently <a id="cg:prime-convolution"></a>$$\label{cg:prime-convolution}
 A(u)=\sum_{\substack{pr=u\\p\in Q_0}}b(r),
 \qquad
 b(r)=W_1(r)\frac{1-\mathcal E(r)}2a^-(r_*),
 \qquad |b(r)|\le L^{B_0},$$ where $B_0$ is fixed by the weight geometry.

<a id="cg:factor-ranges"></a>
Split $p,r$ into dyads of sizes $P,R$. Only $O(L)$ pairs of dyads contribute, and on these $PR\asymp x$. Since $x^{c_1s'}\le p\le x^{c_2s'}$, there is a fixed $c>0$, depending only on Equation <a href="#wt:geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wt:geometry">[wt:geometry]</a>, such that $$\label{cg:factor-ranges}
 P,R\ge x^c$$ for every contributing pair and all sufficiently large $x$. For example any $c<\min(c_1s',1-c_2s')$ suffices.

<a id="cg:primitive-sum"></a>
Every character modulo an odd squarefree $d$ is induced by a unique primitive character $\chi_f$ of conductor $f\mid d$. Write $d=fh$; then $(f,h)=1$ and $\varphi(d)=\varphi(f)\varphi(h)$. For such an induced character, $$\chi(pr)=\chi_f(p)\chi_f(r)
                  \mathbf{1}_{\{(p,h)=1\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{(r,h)=1\}}.$$ The nonprincipal characters have $f>1$; there is no multiplicity in this parametrization. After applying the triangle inequality in Equation <a href="#cg:characters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:characters">[cg:characters]</a>, it remains to bound sums of the form $$\label{cg:primitive-sum}
 \begin{split}
 \sum_{\substack{h\le Q\\h\ {\rm odd\ and\ squarefree}}}
 \frac1{\varphi(h)}
 \sum_{\substack{1<f\le Q/h\\f\ {\rm odd\ and\ squarefree}\\(f,h)=1}}
 &\frac1{\varphi(f)}\sum_{\chi_f\bmod f}^{*}\\
 &\quad\cdot
 \left|\sum_{\substack{p\in Q_0,\ p\asymp P\\r\asymp R}}
       b(r)\chi_f(pr)\mathbf{1}_{\{(pr,h)=1\}}\Psi(pr/x)\right|.
 \end{split}$$ Here and below the asterisk denotes primitive characters. Modulus restrictions may be discarded in nonnegative majorants, which will be useful when applying the large sieve.

<a id="cg:large-sieve-bound"></a>
Choose a fixed $K$ later, and first consider the conductors $f>L^K$. Put $\psi(v)=\Psi(e^v)$ and $\widehat\psi(t)=\int_{\mathbb R}\psi(v)e^{-itv}\,dv$. Fourier inversion gives $$\Psi(pr/x)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}
             \widehat\psi(t)x^{-it}p^{it}r^{it}\,dt,
 \qquad \int_{\mathbb R}|\widehat\psi(t)|\,dt\ll_\Psi1.$$ There is no frequency truncation error. For fixed $h,t$, define $$\mathcal P_h(\chi,t)=
    \sum_{\substack{p\in Q_0,\ p\asymp P\\(p,h)=1}}\chi(p)p^{it},
 \qquad
 \mathcal R_h(\chi,t)=
    \sum_{\substack{r\asymp R\\(r,h)=1}}b(r)\chi(r)r^{it}.$$ Their coefficient squared norms are at most $O(P)$ and $O(RL^{2B_0})$, respectively, uniformly in $h,t$. On a conductor dyad $F<f\le2F$, Cauchy–Schwarz followed by the primitive multiplicative large sieve therefore yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{F<f\le2F}\frac1{\varphi(f)}
       \sum_{\chi_f\bmod f}^{*}
       |\mathcal P_h(\chi_f,t)\mathcal R_h(\chi_f,t)|
 &\ll \frac{L^{B_0}}{F}
       \sqrt{(P+F^2)(R+F^2)PR} \notag\\
 &\ll xL^{B_0}
       \left(\frac1F+\frac1{\sqrt P}+\frac1{\sqrt R}
                        +\frac F{\sqrt x}\right).
       \label{cg:large-sieve-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed $1/\varphi(f)\ll F^{-1}f/\varphi(f)$ on this dyad, giving exactly the large sieve’s primitive-character weight. The masks $\mathbf{1}_{(p,h)=1},\mathbf{1}_{(r,h)=1}$ do not enlarge the coefficient norms.

<a id="cg:large-conductors"></a>
Integrate against $|\widehat\psi|$, sum the conductor and factor dyads, and use Equation <a href="#cg:totient-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:totient-sum">[cg:totient-sum]</a> for the remaining $h$-sum. Since $F\le Q$, Equations <a href="#cg:factor-ranges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:factor-ranges">[cg:factor-ranges]</a> and <a href="#cg:large-sieve-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:large-sieve-bound">[cg:large-sieve-bound]</a> give a total at most $$\label{cg:large-conductors}
 xL^{B_2}
       \left(L^{-K}+x^{-c/2}+x^{\vartheta-1/2}\right)$$ for a fixed $B_2$ independent of $K$. One can use dyads intersected with $f>L^K$, so their lower endpoints are at least $L^K/2$. In particular the summation over $h$ costs a logarithmic factor, not a factor $Q$. Taking $K>B_2+D+2$ makes Equation <a href="#cg:large-conductors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:large-conductors">[cg:large-conductors]</a> $O(xL^{-D})$, because $\vartheta<1/2$.

<a id="cg:small-prime-sum"></a>
It remains to consider $1<f\le2L^K$. For fixed $r\asymp R$, apply Siegel–Walfisz to the nonprincipal character $\chi_f$ on the prime interval $Q_0\cap[P,2P)$. Since $P\ge x^c$, the modulus is bounded by a fixed power of $\log P$. Summing the residue-class estimate against $\chi_f$ and asking for a stronger initial saving absorbs the at most $\varphi(f)$ classes. Partial summation, using the uniformly bounded supremum and total variation of $p\mapsto\Psi(pr/x)$ on this interval, gives, for any fixed $M$, $$\label{cg:small-prime-sum}
 \sum_{\substack{p\in Q_0\\p\asymp P}}
       \chi_f(p)\Psi(pr/x)\ll_{K,M}P L^{-M}.$$ This use of Siegel–Walfisz includes possible exceptional real characters; its potentially ineffective constants cause no problem.

<a id="cg:small-conductors"></a>
Imposing $(p,h)=1$ deletes only $O(1)$ terms in Equation <a href="#cg:small-prime-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:small-prime-sum">[cg:small-prime-sum]</a>: each deleted prime is at least $x^c$, while $h\le x^\vartheta$, so there are at most $\vartheta/c+O(1)$ such primes. Thus the inner double sum in Equation <a href="#cg:primitive-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:primitive-sum">[cg:primitive-sum]</a> is at most $$RL^{B_0}\bigl(PL^{-M}+O(1)\bigr)
 \ll xL^{B_0-M}+x^{1-c}L^{B_0}.$$ The other mask, $(r,h)=1$, only decreases this absolute bound. For the small conductors, $$\sum_{1<f\le2L^K}\frac1{\varphi(f)}
                         \sum_{\chi_f\bmod f}^{*}1\ll L^K.$$ Combining this with Equation <a href="#cg:totient-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:totient-sum">[cg:totient-sum]</a> and the number of factor dyads bounds the small-conductor contribution by $$\label{cg:small-conductors}
 xL^{B_3+K-M}+x^{1-c}L^{B_3+K}$$ for a fixed $B_3$ independent of $K,M$. Choose $M>B_3+K+D+2$. Equation <a href="#cg:small-conductors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cg:small-conductors">[cg:small-conductors]</a> is then $O(xL^{-D})$. Together with the large-conductor estimate and the principal correction, this proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="ex:section"></a>
### Prime extraction

<a id="ex:mass-reminder"></a>
Put $N_u=2u+1$ and $a_x(u)=A(u)\Psi(u/x)$, so that $a_x(u)\ge0$ and $X_A=\sum_u a_x(u)$. Throughout this section the band geometry, the even integer $r_0$, the damping $q_0=1/2$, and $\Psi$ are the fixed data of the weight construction. In particular, Lemma <a href="#wt:mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wt:mass">18</a> gives $$\label{ex:mass-reminder}
 X_A\asymp\frac{x}{L}H_QH_P,\qquad X_A\gg xL^{-C_A},\qquad H_P\ge1.$$ We use the Type II and congruence distribution results proved above, together with the following explicitly stated sieve and proxy inputs.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Sieve and proxy inputs

<a id="stmt-ex-block-sieve"></a>
<div id="ex:block-sieve" class="lemma">

<a id="ex:block-formula"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Block sieve, imported). *Consider finitely many objects with nonnegative weights and, at some primes $p\le z$, designated bad conditions. Suppose that for every squarefree product $d$ of these primes, including $d=1$, the weight on which all conditions indexed by $p\mid d$ hold is $Xg(d)+r(d)$. Here $X\ge0$, $g$ is multiplicative, and, for fixed $\eta_0>0$ and $C$, one has $$0\le g(p)\le1-\eta_0,\qquad
 \sum_{w<p\le w^2}g(p)\le C\quad(w>1).$$ For every sufficiently large even integer $h$, the weight avoiding all the bad conditions equals $$\label{ex:block-formula}
 X\prod_{p\le z}(1-g(p))(1+O(e^{-h}))
 +O\!\left(\sum_{\substack{d\le z^{4h+2}\\d\text{ squarefree}}}
                 |r(d)|\right).$$ The products and sums use only the designated primes. Constants depend only on the two density bounds, and are uniform when $h$ grows. For $h=2$ there is an upper bound by a fixed constant times the displayed main term, plus the same remainder sum.*

*The upper and lower inclusion–exclusion polynomials have coefficients of absolute value at most one, supported on squarefree $d\le z^{4h+2}$. The upper polynomial is nonnegative. Its overcount is bounded by the nonnegative difference between the upper and lower polynomials; that difference also has coefficients of absolute value at most one and the same support bound.*

</div>

This is the Block sieve, Lemma 2.9 of <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[SmoothShifted](#ref-SmoothShifted)]. Its coefficient bound, its growing-$h$ uniformity, and its fixed-depth upper bound will each be used below. No sieve assertion about the present weight is included in the import: its required remainder estimates come from Theorem <a href="#cg:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cg:distribution">22</a> or from the explicit box count below.

<a id="ex:mertens"></a>
Define <a id="ex:euler-products"></a>$$\label{ex:euler-products}
 \begin{split}
 V(y)&=\prod_{p\le y}(1-1/p),\qquad
 V_1(y)=\prod_{3\le p\le y}(1-1/(p-1)),\\
 \mathfrak S&=2\prod_{p\ge3}\left(1-\frac1{(p-1)^2}\right)>0.
 \end{split}$$ Mertens’ estimate and the convergent quotient product give $$\label{ex:mertens}
 V(y)\sim\frac{e^{-\gamma_E}}{\log y},\qquad
 V_1(y)\sim\mathfrak S V(y),$$ where $\gamma_E$ is Euler’s constant.

<a id="ex:proxy-definition"></a>
Here are the proxy estimates we use. For the moment let $0<\kappa<1/50$ and $0<b_1<1/2-\kappa$ be arbitrary fixed numbers, and let $(\gamma,\gamma']$ range over a fixed finite partition of $(b_1,1/2-\kappa]$. On $[x^{b_1/4},x^{1-b_1/4}]$ consider the tests $$I_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)=\mathbf{1}_{m\text{ prime}},\qquad
 I_\gamma(m)=\mathbf{1}_{P^-(m)>x^\gamma}.$$ For a fixed precision $S_1>0$, partition the logarithmic axis into cells of width $\Delta_L=L^{-S_1}$. On every full cell $C_Y=(Y,e^{\Delta_L}Y]$ meeting the ambient interval, put $$\label{ex:proxy-definition}
 c_I(C_Y)=
 \frac{\sum_{m\in C_Y}I(m)}
      {\#\{m\in C_Y:P^-(m)>W\}},\qquad
 B_I(m)=c_I(C_Y)\mathbf{1}_{P^-(m)>W}\quad(m\in C_Y).$$ Even if a later factor range cuts a cell, both counts in this definition are taken on the full cell. Range restrictions are applied only after the ratio has been defined.

<a id="stmt-ex-proxies"></a>
<div id="ex:proxies" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Proxy estimates, imported). *For sufficiently large $x$, the denominators in <a href="#ex:proxy-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:proxy-definition">[ex:proxy-definition]</a> are positive and $0\le c_I\le1$. For every fixed $B>0$, every fixed $S_1>2B+3$, and every interval $K$ inside a dyad in the stated factor ranges, the sequence $$\alpha_m=(I(m)-B_I(m))\mathbf{1}_{m\in K}$$ vanishes outside $P^-(m)>W$, has absolute value at most one, and satisfies <a id="ex:proxy-discrepancy"></a>$$\label{ex:proxy-discrepancy}
 \left|\sum_{m\in J}\alpha_m\chi(m)m^{-1+it}\right|\le L^{-B}$$ for every interval $J\subset[1,x^2]$, every Dirichlet character of modulus at most $L^B$, and every $|t|\le L^B$.*

<a id="ex:prime-density"></a>
*Write $$\mathcal B_\kappa=[x^{1/2-2\kappa},x^{1/2+2\kappa}].$$ There is an absolute constant $C_{\mathrm{pr}}$ such that $$\label{ex:prime-density}
 c_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)\le\frac{C_{\mathrm{pr}}}{LV(W)}
       \qquad(m\in\mathcal B_\kappa).$$ The constant is independent of $\kappa$ and $S_1$; the threshold for $x$ may depend on all fixed parameters.*

<a id="ex:rough-proxy-bound"></a>
<a id="ex:density-integral"></a>
<a id="ex:density-upper"></a>
*For $w>\gamma>0$, set <a id="ex:rough-density"></a>$$\label{ex:rough-density}
 D_\gamma(w)=\frac1w+
 \sum_{l\ge2}\frac1{l!}
 \int_{\substack{t_i\ge\gamma\ (1\le i<l)\\
                   \sum_{i<l}t_i\le w-\gamma}}
       \frac{\prod_{i<l}(dt_i/t_i)}{w-\sum_{i<l}t_i}.$$ This sum is locally finite and nonnegative, and is jointly continuous on compact subsets of $w>\gamma>0$, including the thresholds $w=l\gamma$. If $mn=N_u$ on the support of $\Psi(u/x)$ and $\log n/L\in(\gamma,\gamma']$, then $$\label{ex:rough-proxy-bound}
 c_\gamma(m)\le
 \frac{\sup_{\gamma\le t\le\gamma'}D_\gamma(1-t)+o(1)}{LV(W)}.$$ Here the parameters are restricted to a compact subset of $w>\gamma>0$, as holds for the bins under consideration. For fixed sufficiently small $b>0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_b^{1/2}D_\alpha(1-\alpha)\frac{d\alpha}{\alpha}
     &=D_b(1)-1,\label{ex:density-integral}\\
 D_b(1)&\le\frac{e^{-\gamma_E}}b(1+O(e^{-c/b}))
       \label{ex:density-upper}
\end{aligned}$$ with an absolute $c>0$. The endpoint of the integral is interpreted by its left limit.*

</div>

The discrepancy assertion and the density assertions are, respectively, Lemmas 7.3 and 7.4 of <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[SmoothShifted](#ref-SmoothShifted)], in the section “Extracting primes with smooth predecessors.” These results concern ordinary integer cells; their hypotheses contain no candidate weight. In particular they apply to the present $A$. The exact bound $c_I\le1$ also follows directly because each test selects a subset of the $W$-rough integers in its cell. Thus the coefficient bounds of both $B_I$ and $I-B_I$ do not depend on the precision $S_1$. In <a href="#ex:rough-proxy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:rough-proxy-bound">[ex:rough-proxy-bound]</a>, $$\frac{\log m}{L}=1-\frac{\log n}{L}+O(1/L),\qquad
 \frac{\log m}{L}-\gamma\ge2\kappa+O(1/L),$$ which verifies the required separation from $w=\gamma$. Every little-oh assertion in the proxy estimates is taken only after its parameters have been fixed.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### A uniform positive pair bound

We first prove an upper bound that uses only the original weight geometry. This establishes the constant needed to choose $\kappa$ without referring to $b_1$, the Type II discrepancy exponent, or the eventual proxy precision.

<a id="ex:micro-choice"></a>
Choose a sufficiently large fixed integer $j'$ and a fixed $\theta$ in the gap $$\label{ex:micro-choice}
 c_2s'2^{-j'}<\theta<c_1s'2^{-j'+1},\qquad
 10\theta\le\frac1{20},\qquad
 \sum_{j\ge j'}r_0c_2s'2^{-j}\le\frac1{25}.$$ Such choices exist because $c_2<2c_1$, and all endpoints decrease geometrically. Call $Q_j$ with $j\ge j'$ the micro bands and those with $j<j'$ the macro bands.

<a id="stmt-ex-mask"></a>
<div id="ex:mask" class="lemma">

<a id="ex:assignment-masses"></a>
<a id="ex:positive-majorant"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Assignment and mask majorant). *An assignment $a$ consists of the complete ordered $r_0$-tuples in all the micro bands and one prime mark from each $\mathcal P_g$. Let $D_1=D_1(a)$ be their product, with multiplicities retained, and put $$\lambda_a=\prod_{j=j'}^{j_x}\frac1{r_0!}\prod_g V_g^{-1}.$$ For sufficiently large $x$, $$\label{ex:assignment-masses}
 D_1\le x^{1/20},\qquad
 \sum_a\frac{\lambda_a}{D_1}\ll H_Q,\qquad
 \sum_a\lambda_a\ll x^{1/20}H_Q.$$ For each assignment, ban every odd non-$\mathcal P$ prime at most $x^\theta$ not dividing $D_1$, and independently ban each $\mathcal P$ prime not dividing $D_1$ with probability $1-q_0$. With expectation over this finite random mask, $$\label{ex:positive-majorant}
 A(u)\le A_0(u)\le
 \sum_a\lambda_a\mathbf{1}_{D_1\mid u}\,\mathbb E_a
           \mathbf{1}_{u\text{ is divisible by no banned prime}}.$$ The constants in <a href="#ex:assignment-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:assignment-masses">[ex:assignment-masses]</a> depend only on the fixed weight geometry and $j'$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-mask"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-mask</summary>

*Proof.* The product of the micro entries is at most $x^{1/25}$ by <a href="#ex:micro-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:micro-choice">[ex:micro-choice]</a>. There are $O(T)$ marks and every mark is at most $\exp(L^{2/5})$, so their product is $\exp(O(TL^{2/5}))=x^{o(1)}$. This proves the asserted bound for $D_1$, including assignments with repeated micro entries. Write $$H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}
 =\prod_{j=j'}^{j_x}\frac1{r_0!}
                      \left(\sum_{p\in Q_j}\frac1p\right)^{r_0}.$$ Summing the reciprocal product of the marks gives $\prod_g(V_g^{-1}\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_g}p^{-1})=1$. Consequently $\sum_a\lambda_a/D_1=H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}\ll H_Q$: only finitely many fixed band factors, bounded above and below, have been omitted from $H_Q$. The last assertion in <a href="#ex:assignment-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:assignment-masses">[ex:assignment-masses]</a> follows by multiplying by the bound for $D_1$.

To prove the majorant, fix $u$ with $A_0(u)>0$ and a compatible choice of ordered micro entries and marks. The band gap in <a href="#ex:micro-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:micro-choice">[ex:micro-choice]</a> implies that all non-$\mathcal P$ prime factors of $u$ below $x^\theta$ are among these micro entries, whereas every macro prime exceeds $x^\theta$. All group primes are below $x^\theta$ for sufficiently large $x$. The only random survival conditions are therefore at the distinct unmarked group-prime divisors of $u$. There are exactly $\omega_P(u)-s_P$ of them, so the survival probability is $q_0^{\omega_P(u)-s_P}$, even when marked or unmarked primes occur to higher powers.

Summing one mark per group with normalization $\prod_g V_g^{-1}$ recovers the factor $W_1(u)$. For a fixed prime multiset in any band, the number of ordered $r_0$-tuples divided by $r_0!$ is $1/\prod_p v_p!\le1$. Summing the normalized compatible micro lists retains their exact contribution to $a(u_*)$; dropping the remaining macro-list contribution can only increase it. Thus the compatible assignments already dominate $A_0(u)$. Incompatible assignments contribute nonnegative terms, proving <a href="#ex:positive-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:positive-majorant">[ex:positive-majorant]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ex-box"></a>
<div id="ex:box" class="lemma">

<a id="ex:box-bound"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Positive dyadic pair estimate). *There is a constant $C_{\mathrm{box}}$, depending only on the fixed weight data and the choices in <a href="#ex:micro-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:micro-choice">[ex:micro-choice]</a>, such that, on every dyadic box with $M_1M_2\asymp x$ and $M_i\ll x^{0.54}$, $$\label{ex:box-bound}
 \sum_{\substack{M_1\le m<2M_1,\ M_2\le n<2M_2\\
                 mn=N_u,\ P^-(m),P^-(n)>W}}
       a_x(u)\le C_{\mathrm{box}}X_AV(W)^2.$$ The fixed constants in the size comparisons can be taken to cover all boxes meeting $mn=2u+1$ with $u\in[x,2x]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-box"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-box</summary>

*Proof.* Use Lemma <a href="#ex:mask" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:mask">25</a> and bound $\Psi$ by its fixed supremum. Fix an assignment and a mask. Since $D_1$ is odd, $D_1\mid u$ implies $mn\equiv1\pmod{D_1}$. We count all pairs in the box with this congruence, dropping both the parity of $mn$ and the remaining support restriction on $u$.

For every odd prime $l\le x^\theta$ not dividing $D_1$, impose the bad condition $mn\equiv0\pmod l$ when $l\le W$, and the bad condition $mn\equiv1\pmod l$ when $l$ is banned. There are $2l-1$ pairs of residues with product zero and $l-1$ with product one; the two sets are disjoint. Hence the local number and density of bad residue pairs are <a id="ex:local-density"></a>$$\label{ex:local-density}
 \nu(l)=(2l-1)\mathbf{1}_{l\le W}+(l-1)\mathbf{1}_{l\text{ banned}},
 \qquad g(l)=\frac{\nu(l)}{l^2}.$$ In particular $g(l)\le7/9$ and $g(l)\le3/l$. These give uniform density hypotheses for Lemma <a href="#ex:block-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:block-sieve">23</a>, independent of the assignment and mask.

There are exactly $\varphi(D_1)$ residue pairs with product one modulo $D_1$: choose the first unit and then its unique inverse. This statement does not require $D_1$ to be squarefree. For a squarefree $d$ built from the sieving primes, the Chinese remainder theorem gives exactly $\varphi(D_1)\nu(d)$ pairs modulo $D_1d$, where $\nu$ is multiplicative. Each pair of classes contributes $$\frac{M_1M_2}{(D_1d)^2}
  +O\left(1+\frac{M_1+M_2}{D_1d}\right).$$ Thus the base mass is $M_1M_2\varphi(D_1)/D_1^2$, with local density $g(d)$. Since $\nu(d)\le d\tau(d)^2$, the lattice remainders satisfy $$|r_a(d)|\ll\tau(d)^2\{M_1+M_2+D_1d\}.$$ Use the $h=2$ upper bound in Lemma <a href="#ex:block-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:block-sieve">23</a>, whose level is $D=(x^\theta)^{10}$. Fixed divisor-moment bounds give <a id="ex:box-error"></a>$$\label{ex:box-error}
 \sum_{d\le D}|r_a(d)|
 \ll L^{C_0}\{(M_1+M_2)D+D_1D^2\}$$ for a fixed $C_0$. This includes $d=1$ and is uniform in the mask.

<a id="ex:product-comparison"></a>
We next compare the sieve product with Mertens products. For $l\le W$ banned by the mask, its factor is $1-3/l+2/l^2$; for an allowed $l\le W$ it is $(1-1/l)^2$. For $W<l\le x^\theta$ outside $D_1$, all primes are non-group primes and banned, and the factor is $1-1/l+1/l^2$. Comparing these factors, allowing a convergent product of $1+O(l^{-2})$ and the fixed factor at $2$, gives $$\label{ex:product-comparison}
 \prod_{\substack{3\le l\le x^\theta\\l\nmid D_1}}(1-g(l))
 \ll V(W)^2V(x^\theta)
       \prod_{l\mid D_1}(1-1/l)^{-3}
       \prod_{\substack{l\in\mathcal P,\ l\nmid D_1\\
                         l\text{ allowed}}}(1-1/l)^{-1}.$$ Every prime dividing $D_1$ is at least $L^{20}$, and $\log D_1=O(L)$, so $$\sum_{l\mid D_1}\frac1l
       \ll\frac{L^{-19}}{\log L}.$$ Its correction product in <a href="#ex:product-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:product-comparison">[ex:product-comparison]</a> is therefore uniformly bounded. Independence of the mask gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_a
 \prod_{\substack{l\in\mathcal P,\ l\nmid D_1\\l\text{ allowed}}}
              (1-1/l)^{-1}
 &=\prod_{\substack{l\in\mathcal P\\l\nmid D_1}}
              \left(1+\frac{q_0}{l-1}\right)\\
 &\le\prod_{l\in\mathcal P}\left(1+\frac{q_0}{l-1}\right)
 \le H_P.
\end{aligned}$$ For the last inequality, the group factor in $H_P$ is $$\prod_{p\in\mathcal P_g}\left(1+\frac{q_0}{p-1}\right)
       \frac1{V_g}\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_g}\frac1{p-1+q_0},$$ and the final factor is at least one.

Since $\varphi(D_1)/D_1^2\le1/D_1$, summing the main term over assignments with Lemma <a href="#ex:mask" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:mask">25</a> gives <a id="ex:box-main"></a>$$\label{ex:box-main}
 \ll xV(W)^2V(x^\theta)H_QH_P
 \ll X_AV(W)^2.$$ Here $V(x^\theta)\asymp L^{-1}$, with $\theta$ already fixed, and we used <a href="#ex:mass-reminder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:mass-reminder">[ex:mass-reminder]</a>. It remains essential to sum the errors over assignments as well. From <a href="#ex:assignment-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:assignment-masses">[ex:assignment-masses]</a>, $$\sum_a\lambda_aD_1\le x^{1/10}O(H_Q).$$ Since $D\le x^{1/20}$ and $M_i\ll x^{0.54}$, <a id="ex:all-box-errors"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_a\lambda_a\sum_{d\le D}|r_a(d)|
 &\ll L^{C_0}H_Q
       \{x^{1/20}(M_1+M_2)D+x^{1/10}D^2\}\notag\\
 &\ll L^{C_0}H_Q(x^{0.64}+x^{0.20})
   =o(X_AV(W)^2).\label{ex:all-box-errors}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed $X_AV(W)^2\asymp xH_QH_P/L^2$ and $H_P\ge1$. This proves <a href="#ex:box-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:box-bound">[ex:box-bound]</a>. Every constant used here depends only on the original weight data and $j',\theta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="ex:all-balanced-pairs"></a>
<a id="ex:balanced-constant"></a>
For later use, a dyadic decomposition of $\mathcal B_\kappa$ and Lemma <a href="#ex:box" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:box">26</a> imply $$\label{ex:all-balanced-pairs}
 R_\kappa:=
 \sum_{\substack{mn=N_u,\ m,n\in\mathcal B_\kappa\\
                  P^-(m),P^-(n)>W}}a_x(u)
 \le C_1(\kappa L+1)X_AV(W)^2.$$ There are $O(\kappa L+1)$ first-factor dyads because the interval has logarithmic length $4\kappa L$, and only $O(1)$ second-factor dyads per first-factor dyad because $mn\in[2x+1,4x+1]$. Enlarging partially intersected boxes is legitimate by positivity. For $\kappa<1/50$ all their sides are $O(x^{0.54})$. Consequently $C_1$ is independent of $\kappa,b_1,S_1$. Fix, at this point, the constant $$\label{ex:balanced-constant}
 C_{\mathrm{bal}}=C_{\mathrm{pr}}^2C_1,$$ enlarging it if necessary to absorb the fixed dyadic conventions.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Parameter choice and test replacement

<a id="ex:kappa-choice"></a>
<a id="ex:mesh"></a>
Choose $0<\kappa<1/50$ so small that $$\label{ex:kappa-choice}
 C_{\mathrm{bal}}\kappa<\mathfrak S/4.$$ Next choose $b_1>0$ sufficiently small, with $b_1<1/100$, to satisfy the presieve and subtraction conditions below. The constants in those conditions do not depend on a later proxy precision. Set $$\vartheta=\frac12-\frac\kappa2,\qquad b_* = b_1/3.$$ By continuity in Lemma <a href="#ex:proxies" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:proxies">24</a>, a sufficiently fine fixed partition of $(b_1,1/2-\kappa]$ satisfies $$\label{ex:mesh}
 \sum_{(\gamma,\gamma']}
    \sup_{\gamma\le t\le\gamma'}D_\gamma(1-t)
                      \log(\gamma'/\gamma)
 \le D_{b_1}(1)-1+\frac1{10}.$$ In fact these sums converge to $\int_{b_1}^{1/2-\kappa}D_\alpha(1-\alpha)\,d\alpha/\alpha$: on this fixed compact range, replacing $\gamma$ and $t$ by the same point changes the continuous integrand uniformly by a quantity tending to zero with the mesh. Positivity and <a href="#ex:density-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:density-integral">[ex:density-integral]</a> then give <a href="#ex:mesh" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:mesh">[ex:mesh]</a>.

Prescribe a Type II saving $D_*>C_A+4$ and a congruence saving $D_{\mathrm{cg}}>C_A+4$. Apply Theorem <a href="#ti:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ti:distribution">20</a> with $b_*$ and coefficient bound one, obtaining its required fixed discrepancy exponent $B$. Only now fix $S_1>2B+3$ and define the proxies by <a href="#ex:proxy-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:proxy-definition">[ex:proxy-definition]</a>. All these parameters, including any prime number theorem accuracies in the proxy inputs, are fixed before $x$ tends to infinity.

<a id="stmt-ex-replacement"></a>
<div id="ex:replacement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Replacement on the required factor ranges). *In all factor sums used below, a test $I$ on either factor of $mn=N_u$ can be replaced by $B_I$ with total error $o(X_A/L)$, provided the coefficient on the other factor has absolute value at most one. This holds also when that other coefficient is a proxy.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-replacement"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-replacement</summary>

*Proof.* For the unbalanced sums, the prime factor $n$ is in $(x^{b_1},x^{1/2-\kappa}]$, and the complementary factor lies between fixed positive multiples of $x^{1/2+\kappa}$ and $x^{1-b_1}$. For the balanced sums both factors are in $\mathcal B_\kappa$. Thus every contributing factor dyad, after intersecting with its actual interval, has scale between $x^{b_1/3}$ and $x^{1-b_1/3}$ for sufficiently large $x$. The actual factor ranges also lie inside the ambient proxy interval. The fixed gaps in these exponent inequalities absorb all dyadic endpoint constants.

On a dyad use $(I-B_I)\mathbf{1}_K$ as the first sequence in Theorem <a href="#ti:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ti:distribution">20</a>, where $K$ is the actual summation interval. Lemma <a href="#ex:proxies" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:proxies">24</a> supplies its rough support, coefficient bound, and discrepancy. Exchange the factor names when the test is on the second factor. The theorem requires only boundedness of the companion sequence, so a nonsmooth proxy is permitted. There are $O(L)$ contributing dyadic pairs for each of the fixed finitely many tests. The total error is therefore $O(xL^{-D_*+1})=o(X_A/L)$ by <a href="#ex:mass-reminder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:mass-reminder">[ex:mass-reminder]</a>. The full-cell normalization is retained when $K$ cuts a cell. In particular, neither $B$ nor the coefficient exponent used in the Type II theorem depends on $S_1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Presieving and unbalanced composites

<a id="ex:remainder-sum"></a>
For odd squarefree $d$ write, as in the congruence theorem, $$r(d)=\sum_{d\mid N_u}a_x(u)-\frac{X_A}{\varphi(d)}.$$ Theorem <a href="#cg:distribution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cg:distribution">22</a>, with the already fixed $\vartheta,D_{\mathrm{cg}}$, gives $$\label{ex:remainder-sum}
 \sum_{\substack{d\le x^\vartheta\\d\text{ odd and squarefree}}}
       |r(d)|
 \ll xL^{-D_{\mathrm{cg}}}+X_AL^{-18}=o(X_A/L).$$

<a id="stmt-ex-presieve"></a>
<div id="ex:presieve" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (Initial sifted mass). *For sufficiently small fixed $b_1$, <a id="ex:presieve-bound"></a>$$\label{ex:presieve-bound}
 \sum_{P^-(N_u)>x^{b_1}}a_x(u)
 \ge\frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{L}
       \left\{\frac{e^{-\gamma_E}}{b_1}
                    (1-O(e^{-c/b_1}))+o(1)\right\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-presieve"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-presieve</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#ex:block-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:block-sieve">23</a> to the nonnegative weights $a_x(u)$, with bad condition $p\mid N_u$ at odd primes $p\le z=x^{b_1}$. Its density is $g(p)=1/(p-1)$, at most $1/2$; the sums of these densities over $(w,w^2]$ are bounded. Take $$h=2\left\lfloor\frac1{40b_1}\right\rfloor.$$ Making $b_1$ small ensures that this even integer is sufficiently large for the two-sided sieve. Its level satisfies $$b_1(4h+2)\le\frac15+2b_1<\vartheta.$$ Thus <a href="#ex:remainder-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:remainder-sum">[ex:remainder-sum]</a> supplies the full remainder sum. Since $N_u$ is odd, avoiding the specified odd primes is exactly $P^-(N_u)>x^{b_1}$. Finally use <a href="#ex:mertens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:mertens">[ex:mertens]</a> and $e^{-h}=O(e^{-c/b_1})$ in <a href="#ex:block-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:block-formula">[ex:block-formula]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ex-conditional"></a>
<div id="ex:conditional" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Conditional small-prime sieve). *For each fixed bin $(\gamma,\gamma']$ of the chosen partition, <a id="ex:conditional-sieve"></a>$$\label{ex:conditional-sieve}
 \sum_{\substack{x^\gamma<n\le x^{\gamma'}\\n\text{ prime}}}
       \sum_{\substack{n\mid N_u\\P^-(N_u)>W}}a_x(u)
 =X_AV_1(W)\{\log(\gamma'/\gamma)+o(1)\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-conditional"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-conditional</summary>

*Proof.* Fix such a prime $n$. For each odd squarefree $d$ supported on primes at most $W$, one has $n>W$ for sufficiently large $x$, and $$\sum_{nd\mid N_u}a_x(u)
   =\frac{X_A}{(n-1)\varphi(d)}+r(nd).$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#ex:block-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:block-sieve">23</a> on the objects with $n\mid N_u$, using base mass $X_A/(n-1)$, local density $1/(p-1)$, and $h=2\lceil T^2\rceil$. Its stated growing-$h$ uniformity applies; the level for $d$ is $$D_W=W^{4h+2}=\exp(O(\sqrt L\,T^2))=x^{o(1)}.$$ Every product $nd$ used is at most $x^{1/2-\kappa+o(1)}<x^\vartheta$.

Crucially, the map $(n,d)\mapsto nd$ is injective, since $n$ is the unique prime divisor of $nd$ exceeding $W$. This remains true when the sums are taken over all the disjoint bins. The sieve polynomials have coefficients of absolute value at most one, so the sum of the remainders over all $n$ and $d$ is bounded by the single sum <a href="#ex:remainder-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:remainder-sum">[ex:remainder-sum]</a>, with no divisor multiplicity. It is $o(X_AV_1(W))$, since $V_1(W)\asymp L^{-1/2}$. The main terms are $$X_AV_1(W)(1+O(e^{-h}))
          \sum_{\substack{x^\gamma<n\le x^{\gamma'}\\n\text{ prime}}}
                         \frac1{n-1}.$$ Partial summation from the prime number theorem gives the last sum as $\log(\gamma'/\gamma)+o(1)$, proving the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ex-unbalanced"></a>
<div id="ex:unbalanced" class="lemma">

<a id="ex:surviving-mass"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Removal of unbalanced composites). *The weight of prime values $N_u$, together with composite values whose least prime factor exceeds $x^{1/2-\kappa}$, is at least $$\label{ex:surviving-mass}
 \frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{2L}$$ for sufficiently large $x$ after $b_1$ is chosen sufficiently small.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-unbalanced"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-unbalanced</summary>

*Proof.* If a presieved composite has least prime factor $n\in(x^\gamma,x^{\gamma'}]$, every prime factor of $m=N_u/n$ is at least $n>x^\gamma$. This includes a repeated occurrence of $n$. Its weight is therefore bounded above by the sum over $mn=N_u$ of $I_\gamma(m)\mathbf{1}_{x^\gamma<n\le x^{\gamma'},\ n\text{ prime}}
a_x(u)$. Other divisors counted by this sum merely increase the upper bound.

Replace $I_\gamma$ by $B_\gamma$ using Lemma <a href="#ex:replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:replacement">27</a>, and use <a href="#ex:rough-proxy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:rough-proxy-bound">[ex:rough-proxy-bound]</a>. Because $n>W$, $P^-(m)>W$ is equivalent to $P^-(N_u)>W$; no coprimality between $m$ and $n$ is needed. Lemma <a href="#ex:conditional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:conditional">29</a> and <a href="#ex:mertens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:mertens">[ex:mertens]</a> bound the weight removed for this bin by $$\frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{L}
 \left\{\sup_{\gamma\le t\le\gamma'}D_\gamma(1-t)
                       \log(\gamma'/\gamma)+o(1)\right\},$$ apart from the total $o(X_A/L)$ replacement error. Summing over the fixed mesh and using <a href="#ex:mesh" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:mesh">[ex:mesh]</a> bounds the total removed weight by $$\frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{L}
       \{D_{b_1}(1)-1+1/10+o(1)\}.$$ Subtract this from Lemma <a href="#ex:presieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:presieve">28</a>. By <a href="#ex:density-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:density-upper">[ex:density-upper]</a>, the coefficient of $\mathfrak S X_A/L$ left over is at least $$1-\frac1{10}-O(b_1^{-1}e^{-c/b_1})+o(1).$$ Choose $b_1$ so small that this is greater than $1/2$ for sufficiently large $x$. This choice also satisfies the initial sieve conditions. The surviving presieved values are exactly among the two classes in the assertion, so their weight proves <a href="#ex:surviving-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:surviving-mass">[ex:surviving-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Balanced composites and the conclusion

<a id="stmt-ex-balanced"></a>
<div id="ex:balanced" class="lemma">

<a id="ex:balanced-bound"></a>
**Lemma 31** (Balanced composite bound). *With the constant fixed in <a href="#ex:balanced-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:balanced-constant">[ex:balanced-constant]</a>, the weight of composite $N_u$ with $P^-(N_u)>x^{1/2-\kappa}$ is at most $$\label{ex:balanced-bound}
 C_{\mathrm{bal}}(\kappa+1/L)\frac{X_A}{L}+o(X_A/L).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-balanced"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-balanced</summary>

*Proof.* Such a composite has exactly two prime factors counted with multiplicity: three would give $N_u>x^{3(1/2-\kappa)}>4x+1$. Both factors lie in $\mathcal B_\kappa$ for sufficiently large $x$, since the larger one is at most $(4x+1)x^{-1/2+\kappa}<x^{1/2+2\kappa}$. Ordered prime pairs cover every such composite at least once; distinct primes give two pairs and a repeated prime gives one. Thus their total weight is bounded by $$\sum_{\substack{mn=N_u\\m,n\in\mathcal B_\kappa}}
              I_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)I_{\mathrm{pr}}(n)a_x(u).$$ Use the exact identity $$I_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)I_{\mathrm{pr}}(n)
       -B_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)B_{\mathrm{pr}}(n)
 =(I_{\mathrm{pr}}-B_{\mathrm{pr}})(m)I_{\mathrm{pr}}(n)
   +B_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)(I_{\mathrm{pr}}-B_{\mathrm{pr}})(n).$$ Lemma <a href="#ex:replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:replacement">27</a> applies to the two terms in turn, exchanging factor roles in the second. Both companion sequences have absolute value at most one, independently of $S_1$. The resulting positive proxy sum is, by <a href="#ex:prime-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:prime-density">[ex:prime-density]</a>, at most <a id="ex:prime-to-positive"></a>$$\label{ex:prime-to-positive}
 \frac{C_{\mathrm{pr}}^2}{L^2V(W)^2}
 \sum_{\substack{mn=N_u,\ m,n\in\mathcal B_\kappa\\
                  P^-(m),P^-(n)>W}}a_x(u).$$ Apply <a href="#ex:all-balanced-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:all-balanced-pairs">[ex:all-balanced-pairs]</a> and <a href="#ex:balanced-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:balanced-constant">[ex:balanced-constant]</a>, and add the $o(X_A/L)$ replacement error. This proves <a href="#ex:balanced-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:balanced-bound">[ex:balanced-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-balanced-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-balanced</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Combining Lemmas <a href="#ex:unbalanced" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:unbalanced">30</a> and <a href="#ex:balanced" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:balanced">31</a> gives $$\sum_{N_u\text{ prime}}a_x(u)
 \ge\left(\frac{\mathfrak S}{2}
               -C_{\mathrm{bal}}\kappa-o(1)\right)\frac{X_A}{L}
 \gg\frac{X_A}{L},$$ by <a href="#ex:kappa-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:kappa-choice">[ex:kappa-choice]</a>. Lemma <a href="#wt:squarefree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wt:squarefree">19</a> removes only $o(X_A/L)$ from this mass. Consequently there is a supported $u$ with $2u+1$ prime and $2u$ squarefree.

For completeness, the support condition counts prime factors with their multiplicities. The ordinary part $u_*$ has $r_0(j_x+1)$ prime factors, an even number because $r_0$ is even. The factor $(1-\mathcal E(u))/2$ in $A(u)$ imposes an odd total multiplicity on the group-prime part. Hence $\Omega(u)$ is odd. Every supported $u$ is odd, so $\Omega(2u)=1+\Omega(u)$ is even. On the retained squarefree predecessors this is also an even number of distinct prime factors. Finally $u\in[x,2x]$ wherever $a_x(u)>0$; the resulting primes exceed $x$ for arbitrarily large $x$, proving infinitude. ◻

</details>

The order of choices is intrinsic to the argument: the original weight data and the micro-band choices determine $C_{\mathrm{box}}$ and $C_{\mathrm{bal}}$; then come $\kappa$, $b_1$, the finite mesh, the distribution savings and their discrepancy exponent, and finally $S_1$. All thresholds for $x$ are imposed afterwards. In particular the absolute prime-proxy constant and the coefficient bound one, rather than any precision-dependent regularity, are what make this order valid.
